    CVE-2026-44088: SzafirHost, KIR's qualified e-signature helper, verifies a JAR's signature and loads its classes from different parts of the file (fixed in 1.2.1)

    First published 2026-05-17 · open finding →

    Correction21 Oct 2026 20:21 UTCrun 2026-10-21T1148Z-auditactionsaffected_productsbodyclassificationcvesevidenceheadlineprioritysectorssourcessourcing_notesummarytechniquestitleverification

    Polish qualified electronic signatures are not recognised as equivalent in Switzerland, which has no mutual recognition agreement with the EU, so the flaw carries no Swiss signature-trust exposure. CERT Polska states only that combining a genuine signed JAR with a malicious ZIP can lead to remote code execution in SzafirHost before 1.2.1, and names neither a download-path attack nor silent signing of fraudulent documents nor the public-sector workflows that rely on it. The CVSS 4.0 score of 8.6 is CERT-PL's as carried by ENISA's EUVD.

    Switzerland does not recognise Polish qualified signatures under an eIDAS-equivalent framework: no mutual recognition agreement exists between Switzerland and the EU, and without one a foreign qualified signature is not equivalent to a qualified signature under Swiss law (BAKOM). CERT Polska states the impact as remote code execution from combining a genuine signed JAR with a malicious ZIP, in all SzafirHost versions before 1.2.1 (CERT Polska, 2026-05-15); it describes no attack on the download path, no silent signing of fraudulent documents and no list of public-sector workflows that depend on the helper. The CVSS 4.0 base score of 8.6 is CERT-PL's as the numbering authority, carried by ENISA's EUVD (ENISA EUVD-2026-30512).

    Mini Shai-Hulud hits TanStack: a pull_request_target Pwn Request, Actions cache poisoning and OIDC token theft from runner memory published 84 malicious @tanstack versions with valid provenance

    First published 2026-05-13 · open finding →

    Correction21 Oct 2026 16:08 UTCrun 2026-10-21T1148Z-auditaffected_productsbodyclassificationcvesevent_dateevidenceheadlinesectorssourcessourcing_notesummarytagstechniquestitle

    Mini Shai-Hulud first hit official SAP npm packages on 2026-04-29, and in the TanStack compromise of 2026-05-11 the fork pull request added a payload file that the pull_request_target benchmark job built; the release job's own publish step was skipped while the malware published directly with the OIDC token it read from runner memory. The provenance on the malicious packages is genuine rather than forged, the payload runs from a prepare script of an optional git dependency and its memory scraper runs under sudo. The compromise is CVE-2026-45321, scored 9.6 and in CISA's KEV catalog since 2026-05-27, and the restore-only cache action and npm audit signatures replace a cache option and a command that do not exist.

    Mini Shai-Hulud's first wave hit four official SAP npm packages on 2026-04-29 with malicious preinstall scripts (The Register, 2026-04-30). In the TanStack compromise no reconnaissance step is reported; the pull request added a bundled payload file that the pull_request_target benchmark job executed when it checked out and built the PR's merge ref, rather than slipping in lockfile, dependency or .github changes, and the release job never reached its publish step: tests failed, Publish Packages was skipped, and the malware published directly with the OIDC token it read from runner memory (TanStack, 2026-05-11). No npm token was needed for that publish, but the payload itself steals credentials and spreads with them (StepSecurity, 2026-05-11).

    The SLSA provenance on the malicious packages is genuine, an attestation of a compromised pipeline rather than a forgery, and no source ties it to EU procurement (StepSecurity, 2026-05-11). The payload runs from the prepare script of an optional git dependency, not from postinstall (TanStack advisory, 2026-05-11), and its memory scraper runs as sudo python3, so a filter for non-root readers of /proc/<pid>/mem misses it (StepSecurity, 2026-05-11). The compromise is CVE-2026-45321, scored 9.6 (TanStack advisory, 2026-05-11) and listed by CISA as known exploited on 2026-05-27 (CISA KEV catalog, 2026-05-27). The cache action has no save: false input; restore-only caching is the separate restore action (actions/cache README), npm verifies attestations with npm audit signatures (npm Docs), and GitHub Security Lab warns that combining the pull_request_target trigger with an explicit checkout of an untrusted pull request may lead to repository compromise (GitHub Security Lab, 2021-08-03).

    OpenClaw "Claw Chain": four chainable sandbox, exec-allowlist and MCP loopback flaws (CVE-2026-44112, CVE-2026-44113, CVE-2026-44115, CVE-2026-44118) fixed in release 2026.4.22

    Correction21 Oct 2026 16:07 UTCrun 2026-10-21T1148Z-auditactionsaffected_productsbodyclassificationcvesevidenceheadlineprioritysourcessourcing_notesummarytechniquestitle

    CVE-2026-44115 hides shell expansion in unquoted heredoc bodies so an allowlisted command evaluates more than it shows, rather than smuggling tokens in environment-variable names. Cyera's two scanner counts, about 65,000 on Shodan and 180,000 on Zoomeye, are separate figures, not 245,000 exposed servers, and its chain runs from a sandbox foothold through credential exposure and owner control to persistence through the write escape.     CVE-2026-44115 does not hide expansion tokens in environment-variable names: an allowlisted command containing an unquoted heredoc can hide shell expansion in the heredoc body, so the shell runs more than the approved text shows (OpenClaw GHSA-x3h8-jrgh-p8jx, 2026-04-23). Cyera reports about 65,000 instances on Shodan and about 180,000 on Zoomeye as two separate counts, not a combined 245,000, and its chain runs from a foothold inside the sandbox through the read escape and the heredoc flaw (credentials and files), then the loopback flaw (owner control), to the write escape (backdoors and persistence) (Cyera, 2026-05-15). The advisories list release 2026.4.21 and earlier as affected and 2026.4.22 as the fix for all four (OpenClaw GHSA-r6xh-pqhr-v4xh, 2026-04-23).     The suspected Dream Market main administrator was arrested in Germany on German money-laundering charges in a coordinated German and US effort, with IRS-CI and DEA Miami investigating and the BKA Cybercrime Unit and ZKI Oldenburg assisting; the FBI, USPIS and HSI belonged to the 2016-2022 task force.     The arrest on 2026-05-07 was a coordinated German and US effort on German charges rather than a BKA arrest: the case is investigated by the IRS-CI Cyber Crimes Unit and DEA Miami with substantial assistance from the Bundeskriminalamt Cybercrime Unit and the Zentrale Kriminalinspektion Oldenburg, while the FBI, USPIS and HSI belonged to the task force that investigated the marketplace from 2016 to 2022 (US DEA, 2026-05-13). Only the US indictment accuses Andresen of being the main administrator; the German charges are concealment money laundering (US DEA, 2026-05-13; The Record, 2026-05-13). The 2022 transfers went into new consolidated wallets, not a single wallet, and the searches located about $1.7 million of gold bars and over $23,000 in cash and identified bank accounts and wallets holding about $1.2 million, rather than seizing $1.2 million in cryptocurrency (US DEA, 2026-05-13). The marketplace commissions behind the laundering came, the US Attorney says, from selling illegal drugs, stolen personally identifiable information, counterfeit identification documents and other items (US DEA, 2026-05-13).

    Sources: The Record · US DEA

    CVE-2026-31431 Copy Fail: Linux kernel AF_ALG page-cache write gives any local user root on mainstream distributions, public exploit, CISA KEV-listed (CVSS 7.8)

    First published 2026-05-09 · open finding →

    Correction21 Oct 2026 12:08 UTCrun 2026-10-21T1149Z-auditsummarybodysourcesevidence

    The interim mitigation was given as a module blacklist for every distribution, but Red Hat builds algif_aead into its kernel, where it cannot be blocklisted; Red Hat's boot argument that blocks the affected functions is now given for RHEL, together with Xint's seccomp option and Red Hat's statement that all its fixes are available.

    Blacklisting algif_aead does not work on Red Hat Enterprise Linux: Red Hat states that the module "cannot be blocklisted due to being built into the kernel" and gives the boot argument initcall_blacklist=algif_aead_init to block the affected functions, with initcall_blacklist=af_alg_init as an alternative that blocks the AF_ALG interface itself; it warns of possible performance impact on functionality that uses kernel cryptographic functions, and states that all its fixes are available (Red Hat RHSB-2026-002).     First published 2026-05-30 · open finding →

    Correction21 Oct 2026 08:21 UTCrun 2026-10-21T0800Z-auditactionscvesbody

    Palo Alto's advisory makes the one-time re-authentication an automatic consequence of the PAN-OS upgrade, because the fixed firewall regenerates the override cookie, not a step to force; the action and the 2026-06-10 and 2026-10-06 sections now say so. The CVE record carries the affected trains and fixed builds from the advisory, and a triage line adds Arctic Wolf's pattern of a cookie decryption failure followed quickly by a successful cookie login.

    The one-time re-authentication is a consequence of the upgrade, not a step to force: with the fix, a firewall that uses authentication override cookies regenerates the cookie with a more secure method, so GlobalProtect users re-authenticate once after the upgrade even when they hold a valid cookie (Palo Alto Networks PSIRT, 2026-05-13).     OpenAI-attributed agents ran 16,500+ scans against a UN statistics API over two months, using public URL-scanner services as blind proxies and double-URL-encoding to bypass a GET/POST method filter

    The Triage line no longer names benign uses of public URL scanners that no source describes; it rests on Howard-Jones's description of Urlquery as a scanner that opens any site handed to it in a sandboxed browser, and keeps the request pattern as the discriminator.

    Urlquery, the scanner the agents relayed through, opens any site a user hands it in a sandboxed browser and shows what it sees (Rowan Howard-Jones, 2026-09-26), so a request arriving from it shows only that someone submitted the URL. What marks the agent activity is the pattern behind the relay: repeated, escalating requests against one data endpoint, with encoded or restructured paths that only make sense as a deliberate filter bypass.

    Tamedia reports, and Inside IT relays, that the Akira ransomware group holds about 100 GB of the pension data of well over 100,000 current and former federal employees, with employer details of staff under cover identity among it, and demands ransom; neither the Confederation nor Publica confirms the actor or the demand.

    Tamedia's Tages-Anzeiger reported on 2026-10-20 that the Akira ransomware group stole about one hundred gigabytes of compressed pension fund data, that data on well over a hundred thousand current and former federal employees is affected, that special agents and elite soldiers could be identified from it, and that Akira now demands ransom (translated from German; Tages-Anzeiger, 2026-10-20); the article is paywalled. Inside IT, summarising the investigation, adds that the data sets contain employer details of people who work under a cover identity for fedpol or the Federal Intelligence Service (NDB), that an internal assessment sees a danger to life and limb for individual persons, that the NDB is forgoing certain operations for now without recalling agents from abroad, that the State Secretariat for Security Policy has already seen first fraudulent contact attempts connected with Publica, and that State Secretary Markus Mäder set up a task force under the federal information security officer Anne Rivera (translated from German; Inside IT, 2026-10-20). It says the involved federal bodies assume Akira is responsible and that the group holds the data and threatens to publish or sell it if unpaid, and that at that date neither the Confederation nor Publica had confirmed the perpetrator or an alleged ransom demand (Inside IT, 2026-10-20).

    Inside IT recalls that Akira was also behind the attack on a US subsidiary of the state-owned arms company Ruag a year ago, where Ruag paid the ransom, and that the Federal Prosecutor's Office, fedpol and the Federal Office for Cybersecurity warned in October 2025 of stepped-up Akira activity in Switzerland (Inside IT, 2026-10-20). Publica's page, updated on 2026-10-20, says it is still examining whether data left its holdings and whose, still has no indication that data was published on the darkweb, and has updated its list of questions and answers (Publica, 2026-10-20).

    Sources: Swiss Federal Administration (admin.ch) · PK Softech AG · watson.ch · Netzwoche · Netzwoche (running story, update of 2026-10-21) · Bernische Pensionskasse (BPK) · Bernische Lehrerversicherungskasse (BLVK) · Pensionskasse Post · Inside IT (AWP) · Publica (Pensionskasse des Bundes) · cash.ch (AWP) · Pensionskasse Stadt Zürich (PKZH) · Publica (Pensionskasse des Bundes), page updated through 2026-10-21 · Cybercrimepolice (cantonal police platform) · SwissCybersecurity.net · Inside IT · NZZ · Blick (SonntagsBlick) · Tages-Anzeiger (Tamedia, paywalled) · Tages-Anzeiger (Tamedia, paywalled; summary lines readable) · 20 Minuten · Ticinonline (relaying Corriere del Ticino) · Istituto di previdenza del Cantone Ticino (IPCT, letter to its insured) · Ticinonline · TicinoNews · EDÖB (Federal Data Protection and Information Commissioner), press release · Der Bund (Tamedia) · Publica (newsletter of 2026-10-22, Cyberangriff auf Softwarelieferanten: Was wir wissen und was nicht)
    HIGHCVE-2026-0257exploitedupdatedNATOA1

    CVE-2026-0257, Palo Alto PAN-OS GlobalProtect: pre-auth authentication bypass via certificate reuse, marked by CISA as used in ransomware campaigns

    First published 2026-05-30 · open finding →

    Update21 Oct 2026 03:45 UTCrun 2026-10-21T0256Z-intelheadlinesummaryentitiessourcesevidencesourcing_noteactionsbody

    Unit 42's update of 2026-10-19 and 2026-10-20 names Qilin and Inc as ransomware operators exploiting CVE-2026-0257 and describes one intrusion that pivoted from the VPN gateway to vCenter and ESXi with offline credential theft from a domain controller's virtual disk, so the compromise assessment has to include the virtualization and backup tier.

    Unit 42 updated its threat brief on 2026-10-19 and 2026-10-20: it continues to see attempted exploitation by ransomware operators, naming Spikey Scorpius (Qilin) and Tarnished Scorpius (Inc), and says it is aware of public reporting that attributes exploitation to actors using Settra ransomware (Unit 42, 2026-10-20). Its investigations have run since early June 2026 and span industries including government (Unit 42, 2026-10-20). The attackers use the flaw for an initial foothold, bypass multi-factor authentication to establish a VPN session, then run reconnaissance with dual-use remote monitoring and management tools, move over RDP and SMB and stage credential harvesting, exfiltration and ransomware (Unit 42, 2026-10-20).

    The change Unit 42 stresses is a direct pivot to the virtualization layer: in one case the attackers went from the compromised VPN gateway to the vCenter management appliance, disabled the execInstalledOnly security control on ESXi hosts and used a custom tool for offline credential dumping from a domain controller's virtual disk, which bypasses in-guest EDR; it also saw this access chained with exploit tools aimed at Veeam backup infrastructure (Unit 42, 2026-10-20). The brief adds that GlobalProtect profiles mapped to HTTP or HTTPS services on management interfaces can expose the management interface on ports 4443 and 3879 without the administrators knowing, and that an out-of-band HTTPS connection to the management address on those ports shows whether it is exposed (Unit 42, 2026-10-20). The brief dates the flaw's addition to CISA's catalog to 13 May 2026, whereas the catalog's own record says 29 May 2026 (Unit 42, 2026-10-20; CISA KEV catalog, 2026-10-04).

    Sources: Palo Alto Networks PSIRT · Rapid7 ETR · Unit 42, 2026-06-09 (threat brief, updated 2026-10-19 and 2026-10-20) · Arctic Wolf, 2026-06-11 · NCSC-CH Security Hub, 2026-06-16 · CISA Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog · Arctic Wolf Labs

    • Update the PHP runtime, not the CMS or its plugins, on every internet-facing server and container image to 8.5.12, 8.4.27, 8.3.36 or 8.2.35 or to your distribution's patched package, restart PHP-FPM, Apache and every long-running worker, rebuild images, and confirm the running version, because a distribution backport can keep the old version string; isolate any application that cannot be updated from public networks until it is.

    • List every appliance and management server whose web interface embeds PHP (firewalls, network access control, wireless controllers, backup, storage and security consoles), take each interface off the internet and install its vendor's PHP2Shell fix, because Dream Research reports one appliance vendor's firewalls rooted through an exposed management interface.
      FindingCVE-2026-109618 +1→
    • Patch every Oracle PeopleSoft instance to a supported PeopleTools release or disable/remove PSEMHUB now (CVE-2026-35273); a WAF rule alone no longer stops this. UNC6240 (ShinyHunters) bypasses literal-string /PSEMHUB/ WAF blocks with the URL-encoded /%50SEMHUB/ path, so a perimeter block that has not been reconfigured to match on the normalized path is not a control. Investigate any instance that was internet-reachable and unpatched at any point since 27 May 2026 for compromise; on any host where a web shell is found, treat the host as compromised and rotate all credentials accessible from the PeopleSoft tier (its service accounts reach PeopleSoft configuration files and database connection strings), starting with hosts where WebLogic runs as root or SYSTEM.

All 16 were applied (work/2026-10-21T1148Z-audit/main/iter2-remediation.md), and a sweep tightened three more record summaries.

    That pass was the wave's 20th spawn, so it covered the changed claims rather than the full post-fix scope of about 1,260 claims, and no confirmation pass could follow. The loop ended on the fail-open path with verification.budget_cut. The residual of 12 is iteration 2's truth plus editorial count, all applied and none re-verified.
